The preceding tabulations show that in the period 1932-40,
the Japanese machine tool inventory increased about $236 ullion
of which li2 million (or less) were Japanese production and
$125 million ore imported. By contrast, during this period,
the United States machine tool inventory increased 735 million--
more than three times as much. If estimates are added coyering
1941 and 1942, the Japenese increment probably did not exceed
$80 million, (imports ceased), while the United States inventory
expanded by about $1,800 million, or more than 22 times that of
Japan. For 1943 a further Japanese expansion of some 20 percent
may be estimated. Thus, if Japanese production reached, say,
$35 million in 1941 and $45 million in 1942, production this year
may be expected to run between $50.million and $60 million. This
compares roughly with the figure Y 200,000,000 set as the goal
of the expansion program to be achieved in 1941. The weight of
evidence, including repeated references from Japanese sources to
the difficulties encountered by this program makes a two-year
delay in achieving its objective a reasonable assumption.
The Problems of Precision in Machine Tools. In aircraft
engines and to a lesser degree in tanks, guns, and automotive
equipment, it is necessary that parts be finished to a tolerance
of not over O.O0l inch. For certain parts, the limitations are
much smaller. The Japanese have usually failed in their efforts
to produce machines that will operate within such close limits.

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
-52
As has been indicated, Japanese tool makers, to a great extent;
confined their product to a limited variety of sizes and types
of second-grade standard machine tools, such as are used in
this country in garages, jobbing shops or repair shops. They
have always had trouble with gear-making, depending almost
entirely üpon the United States for gear cutters, hobs and
broaches. Although in these fields there have been many
Japanese attempts to make durable cutting tools capable of
producing satisfactory parts, these efforts met with little
success. The failures were in metallurgy and in the geometry
of cutting. Thus, one of the principal bottlenecks in the auto-
mobile industry and in producing aircraft engines oocurs in the
number of gear-cutting machines available.
The presont Japanese supply of imported machines permits
of no substantial expansion of mass production methods. More-
ovar, the upkeep of machines in operation is notoriously less
efficient in Japan than in wostern countries. Under tho most
favorable circumstances, bofore the Japanesc machine tool in-
dustry would be capable of replacing worn-out machines or
oquipping new plants without substantial loss in officicncy,
a minimum of several years would have to clapse. It will not be
within tho probable duration of the prosent wer, in the opinion
of many American enginccrs.
Mass Production Tochniques. Another critical bottleneck
that would exist in the Japanese automotive and aviation in-
dustries if major changes were made in the direction of mass.
production output, is the inability of the Japanese to make jigs,
fixtures, and dies, and a lack of tool facilities for producing
them expeditiously. Not only are tool-making facilities limited,
but there is a dearth of trained personnel that can design
such equipment and work to the exacting clearances required.
If a well-designed fixture requiring a minimum of time to trans-
fer tho work is used, production can be maintained at a maximum,
but if this fixture or jig is of poor design, the production
capacity of the mechino will be affe cted accordingly.
It is also necessary to emphasize the importance of
perishable cutting tools and hand machines. Regardless of the
high produotivity of a powor-driven machine tool when properly
oporated and equipped, such a machine will prove no more
officient than the. eutting tools with which it is provided. For
example, a machine may be capablo of very high speeds end feeds
resulting in rapid production, but if low-grade cutting tools
or poorly heat-trcated tools instead of. tungsten carbide tools
are used, then the speeds and fceds mus t be cut to conform to
the low-grade tooling. In terms of production, the difference

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
6
-53
between a carbon steel outting tool and a tungeten carbide
eutting tool is the difference between fifty feet and 300
feet per minute. The Japanese have little experience in the
manufaoture of these higher speed cutting tools. Although
imported machine tools were squipped with high speed cutting
tools, these cutting tools are perishable and the Japanese had
no opportunity to accumulate stocks. Thus, the rate of produc-
tion of their best power-driven machine tools will depend to a
large extent on their ability to attain production of effective
outting tools.
Anti-Friotion Bearings. The importance of ball and roller
bearing production was very belatedly recognized in Japan, with
the result that reliance on foreign bearing products increased
with the growth of home production in machinery, automobiles,
and airplanes. In 1940-41 it was estimated that, as a result
of strenuous efforts by the army and navy, total Japanese
anti-friction bearing production of all types (roller and ball)
was about 55,000 per day. In addition to this, SKF was furnish-
ing Japan with approximately 10,000 pieces per day. Under
present schedules in the Japanese automotive, airplane and
machine tool industry, it has been estimated that 65-70,000
units per day would be needed, inclusive of service replace-
ments. It would appear that this over-all quantitative
requirement is being met. The above estimates are, of neces-
sity, approximations, but they point to an output probably
sufficient to cover the number of pieces necessary, not only
for production purposes, but also for replacements. The
quality of the output, however, is a different matter.
Anti-friction bearings fall roughly into two categories:
preoision and ordinary bearings. The manufacture of even
ordinary bearings of good quality is a complicated and diffi-
cult job. The manufactures of fine precision bearings, whose
tolerances are on the order of two to eight ten-thousandths
of an inch, is an art which has been mastered by only a few
firms in the world. It is particularly difficult to produce
precision bearings in either very small or very large sizes.
Although SKF carried in stock in Japan from 1,800 to 2,000
types, the Japanese, in order to step up their over-all pro-
duction, decided to concentrate on popular sizes running up
to l00 mm., but not including the small bearings required for
such equipmont as magnetos, signal apparatus and the like. It is
believed-that any attempt on their part to make all the sizes
required would reduce their over-all production below their
estimated quantitative requirements. As late as October 1941,

Ings of industrial personnel. The increasing domands
Mms of trained labor. The Japanese are skillful artisans
Faoilities for training new workors while maintaining full
industrial production are limited, however, and in the absence
of a tradition of mass production the difficulties inyolved
are magnified. It takes longer to develop competent workers.
There is an ospecial shortage of supervisors at the shop fore-
man level. Moreover, the supervisory class as a whole is less
efficient and less competent than are the workers. Japanese
technical engineers are frequently highly trained, but more
specialised than those in the West and hence less versatile.
Even in the same industry they cannot be shifted so'easily
from one branch of production to another. Foreign technicians
especially German and Swedish advisers who may be present in
Japan, will find difficulty in duplicating their European
practice because Japan is relatively so much newer to modern
industrial technique.
The strict caste system prevailing in Japanese indus-
try constitutes another obstacle. The mechanic with more
than average technical knowledge cannot be advanced to a
position of authority. The technical engineer is prevented
from obtaining the practical experience required to'qualify
him for the position of supervising.a factory. Management is
frequently uninformed on technical problems. There is thus a
wide gap between the technical and practical sides in the
management of Japanese. industries. This gap is an important
reason for the inefficiency so often noted in the management
of Japanese industries, and in the present critical period its
effects are another limiting factor in the expansion of
armament production.
The following sections deal with the present position
and future potential of the Japanese armament industries in
view of limitations indicated in the preceding pages.
Aircraft Production
Analysis of data concerning the Zero type single seater
fighters indicates that initial production was started in a
single plant late in 1940. The production rate appears to
have climbed by stages to a poak at the end of 1941 of about
60 planes per month. At this point a second plant came into
production but the initial producer appears to have begun a
process of converting over to a new mödel of the same type.
The loss of production during this conversion process was
not fully compensated for by the production of the added
facility and the combined rate during the second quarter

=== Page 181 === (confidence 0.99)
-56-
 below that preyailing at the close of 1941. The third
ter. however, began to show the effects of the combined
ffort and by the end of 1942 the Zero single seater fighters
pe being produced at the rate of about 150 per month, with
an additional 35 Zero planes of the float type.
It is believed that the Japanese have had relatively.
greater suocess in achieving fair production rates in single
engime fighters than in other types. Such evidence as there
is at hand suggests that difficulty has been encountered in
the production of multi-engined bombers, and the apparent
reluctanoe of the Japanese in the field to risk bomber losses
to an extent comparable to their frequency free use. of
fighters tends to confirm the evidence on production.

Development and Organization of the Industry
The Japanese aircraft industry is comparatively young.
Lacking experience it has relied upon foreign technical
assistance for development, at first principally French and
British, and later American and German. Lacking strong sup-
porting and related industries, it has had to develop these
concurrently.
Japanese aircraft and aircraft engine design reveals the
heavy dependency upon foreign teehnical knowledge that has
characterized the growth of the industry. Imitations and

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
57
adaptations predominate. This is not always or necesserily a
signor weakness, however, for some of the results have been
deoadaday efficient.
Organisationally, the Japanese have tended to favor cen-
tralization of assembly within relatively few companies, but
dispersal of production of components and sub-assemblies among
a great number of companies. At the same time, and probably
as part of the same general plan, there has been a relatively
high degree of standardization of plane design. For instance,
the famous Zero-type fighter is usod as a land-based fighter,
as a ship-borne fighter, and as a float-type fighter.
The present organization of the aircraft industry in
Japan is directly the result of government planning and direction.
From early paternalism toward the industry, the government has
rapidly advanced to outright oontrol.
In 1939, 14 companies, headed by Mitsubishi Heavy Indus-
tries and Nakajima Airplane Mfg. Co. were licensed by the govern-
ment for the manufacture of aircraft, airframes, engines and
components. In August 1939, the government granted the Kanega-
fuchi Spinning Company permission to establish a fifteenth
company - the Nippon Kokusai Koku Kogyo K.K. In addition to
these private companies, the Japanese government assembles,
repairs, and produces components at a number of Army and
Naval arsenals.
In 1939 the Japanese Government enforced the National
Mobilization Law adopted March 24, 1938, and assumed control
of the aircraft industry. In December 194l, the Airoraft
Manufacture Ordinance was revised, and in addition to govern-
ment control over the manufacture, of aero-engines, airframes
and propellers, the new plan included control over all manu-
facturers carrying on production of parts.
At present there are probably well in excess of 200
sompanies contributing significantly to Japanese aircraft
produotion. 209 companiés are reportéd as regularly regis-
tered members of an industrial association formed in late
1942 of concerns producing aircraft and parts for the Army.
At least 140 companies and 175 factories have been identified
by intelligence sources as producing planes or components
for the Japanese armed forces.

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.98)
58
While Japanese aircraft production is definitely
greater and more advanced than was generallyrecognised
before Pearl Harbor, continuing expansion will be possible
only if many diffioulties are overcome, No single, out-
standing bottlenecks are known to exist. Produation limi-
tations appear generally to be those arising from the
problems involved in the development and expansion of mass
production in a highly technical industry without.the sup-
port of well-established industries in related and support-
ing fields.
Tanks and Trucks
Japan's automotive industry, like its airplane in-
dustry, is of comparativoly recent developments. Signifi-
cant progress in domestic output was not achieved until
after 1937-38. By 1940 total production of motor vehicles
is estimated to have reached 23,525 units, of which 23,450
were trucks. Included within this latter figure would be
Japan's output of armored vohicles.
Based on.estimated capacity, total production of
trucks and armored vohieles is not belioved to have ex-
ceeded 50,000 units: for 1942 or 11kely to exceed 60,000
units in 1943. The number of armored vehicles included
within this total is not known. The Japanese army, how-
ever, is thought to have 85 meohanized divisions, supported
by 15 units of special tank brigade forees. These divi-
sions and tank brigade units would require 4,400 light and
medium tanks and 7,300 motorcycles with side cars, in addi-
tion to trucks and cars
The combinod requiremonts for tanks and other motor

Military Reguirements
War Department estimates for the numbers engaged in the
armed forces of Japan as of December 31, 1942, are as follows:
Army (including Army Air Corps)
2,750,000
Navy (including Naval Air Force)
325.000
Total
3,075,000
According to estimates in the foregoing table, some
920,000 males can be released from civilian employment in
1943. Of these, some 425,000 wil1 be needed to increase
the employment in war industries, thus leaving 495,000
available for the armed forces. The addition of this number
to those already in the armed forces at the end of 1942,
gives a total of 3,570,000 for 1943. Estimates of casual-
ties for the past five years have averaged 200,000 a year.
Assuming that casualties for 1943 will be met from last
year's conscripts, and that these, in turn, will be replaced.
up to about 300,000 by the annual male increment to the popu-
lation, the 495,000 new males released from industry would
represent a potential net increase to the Japanese armed
forces.
Labor Reserves
Males. Male labor is believed to be fully utilized.
Estimates of Japanese population and employment show that
the labor force already'contains in excess of 1,000,000
male workers under 15 and over 65 years of age.
Females. An employable reserve of about 3 million
women was believed to exist at the close of 1942. Japan
may draw on about 110,000 from this reserve to replace
males in the coming year. To replace these, in turn, there
will be the annual increment of about 300,000 from the
growth in population. Of these, many will never enter the
labor market. A continuing reserve of about 3,000,000
women in 1943 seems, therefore, to be likely.
Industrial Expansion
Large withdrawals for military service during and
after 1937 coincided with a tremendous expansion in heavy
industry. Between 1931 and 1940 the general index of
industrial production (all industrios) increased 100 per-
cent. Production of consumers' goods increased only

Sphools . The discrepancy in the engineering field between

Training Programs and Labor Mobilization
Since that time, however, Japan has taken steps to organ-
ize manpower and to train and allocate it for maximum efficiency
in war. The first step, after registration of all civilians in
August 1938, was the passage of an Ordinance compelling the train-
ing of technicians and skilled workers. Under this Ordinance all
important private industries were required to give 2 to 3 year
training courses following detailed government presoription and
under close government supervision. In addition to these, govern-
ment training schools were also set up to meet the need.
Soon thereafter, employment of all workers in war industries
was restricted and made dependent upon consent of the government
employment offices. Then followed, between July 1939 and Septem-
ber 1940, the National Civilian Conscription Act, making males
between 15 and 50 in designated (war) industries subject to labor

VII. PROBLEMS OF WARTIME ECONOMIC ADMINISTRATION
Summary
During the.latter half of 1942 a continuous series of
readjustments in economic administration reflected Japan's
efforts to meet the demand for greatly increased arms pro-
duction. In January 1943 radical proposals vesting much
greater powers in Premier Tojo were submitted to the Diet
in ordinance form. By February, while the ordinances were
still being railroaded through the Diet, a specific program
affecting the key industries from which greater production
is required in the current stage of the war was being ef-
fectuated.
Five so-called "main" or "nucleus" industries--aircraft,
shipbuilding, iron and steel, coal mining, and the light
metals--have been given priority status with respect to
materials, labor, capital, power, and transport facilities.
Operational authority over these key industries is being
centered in the Cabinet under Tojo's immediate direction.
This program of "planned production" thus involves a radical
centralization of administrative controls previously divided
between the Cabinet Planning Board, the Ministries, and the
Industrial Control Associations.
Difficulties in administrative coordination which pre-
vailed in 1942 will be largely remedied under this new system.
Production in the key spheres of iron and steel, coal, ship-
building and aircraft will thereby be accelerated in 1943, thus
adding a further increment to Japan's economic potential.
Nature of the Administrative Struggle
At the beginning of 1943 the Japanese were engaged in
a radical overhaul of their system of economic administration,
looking towards more centralized state control and operation
of industry. Tojo himself, representing the Army, was seeking
direct operational authority over the basic war industries,
including iron and steel, coal, and chemicals. Resistance
to this effort by the industrial leaders had created a minor,
and.probably temporary, crisis in wartime administration.
Underlying this struggle was a crisis in war production,
mainly arising from the enormously increased tasks laid upon
Japanese-industry. The basic problem of vastly expanding
shipping and armaments production against relatively inflexi-
ble limits. of plant equipment, machine tools, and trained

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
-72元
personnel had passed into an emergency stage. These concrete
difficultiee in the production sphere were intensified by a
lack of effeotive centralization in the administration of
Japan's wartime economy. Administrative powers were scat-
tered through a half dozen Cabinet Ministries, inadequately
coordinated by the Cabinet Planning Board. A unified polioy
was thus laoking at the top, within the Cabinet itself.
Under such conditions it was impossible for the Cabinet to
give centralized direction to a newly organized set of In-
dustrial Control Associations, which exercised semi-sutonomous
powers within tho field of industrial management and operation.
The program submitted to the Diet by Premier Tojo in
January 1943 was mainly designed to ovorcome this administra-
tive disunity. Under the program, Tojo will be given author-
ity to issue economic directives to any of the Ministries and
to transfer administrative functions from one Ministry to
another. Tojo will thereby be empowered to assign to a single
Ministry, probably the Ministry of Commerce and Industry,
centralized authority over the management of the key war in-
dustries. To facilitate this transition, the industries so
affected will be directly taken over by the state, under
powers conferred by the National Mobilization law.
An internal struggle of considerable proportions is
occurring while the new system is being put into effect. Once
the change is made, however, it may be expected that a much
more effective administration of wartime production will result.
During 1943 this improved administrative system will materially
assist Japan's efforts to increase production, thus adding a
new increment to Japan's war potential.
Background of the Crisis
The full scope and magnitude of the economic problems
confronting Japan have emerged only during the past six months.
The initial tasks of the war, primarily involving conquest of
Southeast Asia and the Southwest Pacific archipelagoes, were
successfully accomplished by utilization of the stacks of
finished munitions and the s hipping tonnage accumulated during
previous years. In the summer of 1942, with the beginning of
the United Nations' counter offensives in the Solomons and
New Guinea, much greater economic tasks had to be faced.
On the home front, industrial production had to be raised
to new levels. The output of basic industry, especially iron
and steel, coal, aluminum and copper, had to be increased.
Industrial processing capacity had to be rapidly expanded in
combat armament (notably aircraft) and.shipbuilding.

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
-73-
yActivities in the newly occupied territories had to be
geared Into this domestic program. Only the barest minimum
 capital goods equipment could be allotted to: the exploita-
tion of the southern regions. Developmental projects had to
be striotly adapted to the immediate requirements of Japan's
hame industry. Economic activity in the south was therefore
mainly directed toward the rapid extraction of certain key
mihersls and agricultural products in which Japan was defi-
ient and for which equipment and labor power were largely
available on the spot. Machinery and tools were supplied
to relatively few enterprises, among which the most vital
were oil processing, extraction of a few of the key metals
(lead, zinc, chrome and nickel), and possibly shipbuilding
at Hongkong and Singapore.
Administrative Differentiation: Outer Zone vs. Inner Zone
These factors set the pattern for the major changes in
Japan's administrative structure which have occurred during
the war. Careful limitation of offort was required in the
south, rapid expansion was needed in tho north.
Outer Zone. The military governments in the south
have meticulously regulated the degreeof exploitation per-
mitted by the Japanese business interests and carefully
limited the flow of trained personnel into this area.
Establishment of the Greater East Asia Ministry on November 1,
1942 was essentially aimed at confirming the rigid control
over economic policy in the Outer Zone (mainly the southern
territories) already being oxercised by the local military
administrations. The Army was determined to prevent a flood
of private invostors and ontrepreneurs from sweeping over
the southern regions. It was keenly aware that no compre-
hensive long-term development of the new southern conquests
could be permitted while the issue of the war was still un-
decided. It was intent on one aim--subordination of exploi-
tative and developmontal activities to the requirements of
war.
Tho local southern military administrations could not
of themselvos assure the attainmont of this aim. Authority
over the broad lines of Ministerial policy in Tokyo had
to be obtained. The. Greator East Asia Ministry, vested
with over-all administrative powers in occupied territories
and dominated by the Army, was 'astablished in order to nail
down this authority and make it secure. Jurisdiction of
this Ministry extended to Manchuria; Occupied China, South-
east Asia and the Southwest Pacific, i.e., the torritories

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
-74-
ogcupied since September 18, 193l. The old-line Ministries:
me authority over the pro-1931 Japanese Empire (Japan
roper, Korea, Formosa, Karafuto, and the Mandated Ielande).
The Inner Zone. Thaactual economic line of division,
it should be omphasized, differs from this formal administra-
tive line of division. The essential Inner Zone, which Japan
must retain at all costs if its war economy is to function
effectively, includes Japan Proper, Karafuto, Korea, Manchuria,
North China, and Formosa.
The central problem of wartime economic administration
did not lie in the southern territories. It lay rather in
the Inner Zone and particularly on the home front in Japan
Proper, where the munitions industry is largely concentrated.
Here the task ofmising productive capacity to new levels
has proved complex, as indicated by a steady series of
administrative readjustments. The year 1942 was marked by
the formation of Control Associations in all fields of
economic activity and by the increased powers assigned to
the Cabinet Planning Board. These devices for unifying and
centralizing the 'administration of war-time production were
still inadequate. In January-February 1943, as already
indicated, several basic industries were being subjected to
government control and operation, while Tojo was seeking Diet
approval for a set of unprecedented powers designed to con-
centrate full authority over wartime economic administration
in his hands. A complex group of administrative problems
lay behind this series of readjustments.
Administrative Problems on the Home Front
Japan's war economy is planned and administered not by
special agencies but by the Cabinet headed by General Hideki
Tojo." (See Chart). Over-all programs are initiated by the
Cabinet Planning Board but are subject to Cabinet approval.
Assignments of production schedules to the Control Associations
are made by the competent Ministers. In order to make clear
the difficulties which have arisen the roles played by these
agenoies must be examined in reverse order: (1) the Control
Associations, (2) the Planning Board, and (3) the Ministries.
The Control Associations. Since the autumn of 1941, the
greater part of Japanese industry has been'marshaled into
semi-official cartels. The ordinance empowering this program,
known as the Major Industrial Organizations Control Ordinance,
was approved on September 1, 1941. By June 1942 Control Asso-
ciations had been formed for each key industry such as coal,
iron, machinery and chemicals, Presidents of these Associations

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
-75
wore designated by the competent Minister and could be removed
 uhaar thé authority of a competent Minister of State; most
di of the Associations were responsible to the Minister of Com-
morce and Industry, the Minister of Finance, the Minister of
Communications, or the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry.
(See Chart in Appendix). Over-all production schedules,
drafted by the Cabinet Planning Board, were assigned to the
 Control Association by the competent Minister. Each Asso-
ciation was then responsible within its field for control
of production, the supply of funds, material and labor re-
quired, and the allocation of schedules among its member
enterprises.
This streamlining of economic administration had ob-
vious advantages. It helped to simplify the problem of
scheduling production under emergency wartime requirements.
Cabinet Ministers or members of the Cabinet Planning Board,
in framing schedules and assigning quotas, were able to deal
with broad over-all fields of industry, headed by a small
group of business executives in the Control Associations.
Ultimate responsibility for production, once the schedules
were fixed, was placed on the. Control Associations.
On the other hand, in actual operation the system
revealed important drawbacks. It represented a compromise
between the Army and big business which toned down the
former's demand for strict government control. The Control
Associations were organized essentially on lines proposed
by the business monopolies (the Zaibatsu). They retained
a large degree of autonomy in management. .The various
Ministers holding economic portfolios, moreover, might
be inclined to give greater veight to the views of the
Control Associations than to the demands of tho Army.
Under these conditions, it is difficult to assess the
extent to which a "business-as-usual" attitude may have.
prevailed in the Control Associations.
More significant was the lack of effective centraliza-
tion of administrative authority. The Planning Board drafted
the over-all production schedules, but these schedules had
to be approved and formally assigned to the Control Associa-
tions by the Ministries. Three different Ministries (Commerce
and Industry, Communications, Agriculture and Forestry) were

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
-76-
elved in this process, while ordnance and naval ship-
lhing were handled by factories directly controlled by
the Planning Board, the Ministries, and the Control
Associations created'possibilities for delay and friction.
The semi-autonomous powers of the various Control Associations
permitted competition for raw materials and manpower which the
several different Ministries could not themselves control.
These serious limitations on efficient administration
of war production did not immediately become apparent. An
indication of the difficulties encountered was the fact that
in May 1942 the aluminum industry was subjeoted to full state
control. Details in this case are lacking but, judging from
later developments, the operational autonomy of the industry
was abrogated in favor of direct administration by a government
department, presumably the Ministry of Commerce'and Industry.
Toward the end of 1942 a more serious, amd much more
general, crisis in the administration of the war economy
developed. By this time the necessity for a large-scale
expansion of munitions production to meet the demands of a
long war had become urgent. The weaknesses of the existing
system of economic administration had meanwhile become so pro-
nounced that drastio changes were seen to be necessary. At
the outset the problem was tackled' through the Cabinet Planning
Board.
Planning Board. The Cabinet Planning Board was, in effect,
an instrumentality of the Army, designed since 1937 to function
as an economic general staff and industrial mobilization agency.
But, though its powers had gradually increased, especially
through assumption of the authority to draft over-all produotion
schedules, the Planning Board had never been able to set aside
the ultimate control over the national economy exercised by
the various Ministries. It considered bills drafted by the
Ministries, prepared opinions on such bills, and could itself
draft próposed legislation, but its actions were not binding
on the government. In all cases, its: recommendations had
first to obtain Cabinet approval before being put into effect.
It had largely influenced the drafting of the National Mobili-
zation Law in 1938, but in 1940-1941 its program for outright
state control of industry had been set aside in favor of the
Control Associations' system proposed by the Japan Federation
of Industry and the Japan Chambers of Commerce. In recent
years, its most important function has been the drafting of
production sohedules--which, however; were subject to approval
and enforcement by the Ministries.

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
77-
The serious problem existing at the end of 1942 was first
met by an attempt to strengthen the powers and coordinating in-
flmmnce of the Planning Board, A Committee for the Inorease
and Strengthening of Emergency Production was set up within
the Cabinet in November. It was composed of representatives
from each Ministry and its chairman was Lieutenant-General
Teiiohi Suzuki, chief of the Planning Board. The Committee
was empowered to formulate and put into execution plans to
increase emergency production of strategic commodities. This
program was to be achieved by adjusting and unifying the ad-
ministrative duties of the various Ministries with respect to
labor, materials, transportation, vital consumption goods and
the enterprises concerned with same. To facilitate application
of the program, regional Deliberative Coordinating Councils
based upon local administrative areas and composed of members
of the various district offices of each Ministry were established.
The first meeting of the new Committee was held in mid-December
1942, when plans were discussed for improving the distribution.
of materials and enhancing productive capacity. By this time,
however, it had already become apparent that the new Committee
would not be able to meet the demands of the situation. Much
more radical moves, revoaled at the Diet session in January,
were already being prepared. These moves had been foreshadowed
on November 20, when the aircraft industry was placed under
direct administrative authority of the War Ministry.
Direct Government Control of Key Industries. Important
steps in the direction of full state control of certain key
industries (in addition to aluminum and aircraft) were an-
nounced at the end of January. The industries involved were
iron and steel, coal and chemicals. In the latter case·it
was stated that the Commerce and Industry Minister would con-
solidate "167 medium and small (chemical) enterprises into
a ..... cooperative and strong enterprise," and would. "carry
.on a comprehensive management centered around a large-scale
factory with superior facilities.and techniques".  These
moves evidently envisaged unrestricted government appointment
of the exccutives in the industries concerned as well. as
wholesale invasion of the sphere of management and operation.
Such a program, applied to the great iron and steel and coal
industries, was far different from tho case provided by the
relatively small and compact aircraft industry.
To put this radical program into effect, Premier (and
War Minister) Tojo evidently felt that even greater adminis-
trative powers had to be placed in his hands. To this end,
two ordinances were framed and submitted to tho Diet for
approval.

VIII FAOPCRS AFFECTING: JAPANIS FUTURE ECONOMIO POLICIES
Japan is in the midst of a transition from a policy
of expansion and conquest to a policy of consolidation and
defense. This shift is not due to a present weakmess or to a
preepeotive deoline in Japan's economio war potentials In
fect, it is probable that Japan's capacity to produce combat
armament will expand throughout 1943. It is due rather to the
fact that Japan's relative position appears to be weakening
because of (1) the expanding power of the United Nations
(particularly in respect to naval construction in Japan as com-
pered to that of the United States), (2) to the apparent
weakening of the German position, and the possibility of a
defeat of Germany which would leave Japan alono against the
world.
These future considerations are influencing curfent
Japanese cconomic policy. On the one hand Japan is making
preparations to hold on to areas presontly possesscd as long
as possibloj: on the other hand, provision is being made against
the possibility that these aroas cannot be held. Japan is,
therefore, attempting to build the most complete cconomio.1
self-sufficienty, possible within an inner zone of defense.
Economic Factors Affecting Offonsive Policies
This analysis should not be interprotod to mcan that Japan
lacks offonsive power at the present time. It means rather that
military factors have become relctively moro important and
economic factors loss important in the dctermination of Japan's
offensivo policy now that the noeded raw materials cro in
Japaneso hands.
China. Insofar as cconomic considerations do influonce
policy, however, it may be said that the strongcst inducoment
to offensive action lies in an offort to defeat China. China is
the key to the economic consolidction of Japan's position, if
not to its further oxpansion. The complote dofeet of China
would roduce tho oconomic drain which gucrilla warfare and tho
maintenanco of a large active army in China entails. It vould
also mako possible a much more offcctivo oxploitation of the
rosources of China which Japen already possosscs.
India. Tho sccond most important oconomic inducoment to
offensivo action is in northcastorn India. The trianglo formed
by Caloutta, Jamshodpur and Burnpur constitutos tho industrial
heart of India. Threo-fourths of all wer production including
noarly all of tho munitions plants are locatod hcro.

Siberia. The immediate economic inducements to offensive

Gentinental and Island Air Bases to Consolidate
Japanls Economio Position
Under existing conditions there is a logical trend from
sea power to land based air power as a basis tor the consolidation
and defense of Japanls present empire. Such a system would be
mado up of a network of air baaes extending down the Asiatio
Coast and dotting the islands of the Southwest Paoific: Its
complete development would require an inland rail transport
system extending along the Asiatic coast from North China to
Singapore arranged so as to give maximum support to such air bases.
It would also require a sufficient development of machine shops,
produotive facilities and assembly plants to provide the equip-
ment necessary for their maintenance.
To complete such a program would require a greater economic
offort than Japan can make in 1943. Many steps have been and
can be undertaken, however, which will yield immediate advantages
that make them worthwhile. A program of this sort tends to
emphasize tho importance of offonsive action in China in order to
consolidate Japan's continental position and to make it possible
to develop a ganuine continental rail aystem from Manchuria to
Singapore.
Tho Prospective Increase in Japan's Economic
Potontial During 1943
Japan possesses raw materials in quantitios potontially
sufficient to employ from two to three times the prcsent volume
of Japanese shipping and processing capacitics.
Bocausc of limitations in these latter rospocts, howover,
the actual overall incrcasc in Japanese productive capacity
during 1943 will be only a small fraction of this potontial.
It is ostimatod, for example, that tho steel and thc power
output of Japan will incrcasc only by about 10 porcent during the
curront yoar. Sinco most of this incrcase is dovoted to combat
armament, honever, incrcases in the production of war material
vill be considerably groater. The production of aircraft prot
bably will incroaso about 25 percent, tanks and trucks 16 per-
pent, ordinances 22 porcent, locomotives 10 percent and freight
bars 15 percont.
The Economic Position of Japan as Compared
With Other Major Powors

are considerably smaller than those of any other major

a basis for comparison in the three laading catcgories of steel,
pal and eleotrical power. The great superiority in the basic

United States alone indicates how rapidly Japan will be out-
distanced in these respects during the current year. It is esti-
mated, for example, that the United States will construct over
19,000,000 deadweight tons of new shipping during 1943 whereas
Japan will probably construct not more than 650,000 gross tons
(appraximately 1,000,000 deadueight tons). The total aircraft
production in Japan during the current year will approximate
9,000 planes of all types wheroas the United States vill produco
approximately 100,000 planes. Similar differences are to be
found in naval cons truction, tanks and trucks, and ordnance.
The following factors, however, largoly offset those
advantages of the United Nations:
(1) Four to five times as much economic potcntial is being
devoted to the European theatre as is being dovoted to the Far
Eastern theatre of action.
(2) Tho shipping of the United Nations is insufficient to
make available combat armament fully offective at those points
where it is required.
(3) Japan has been stockpiling combat armament for over a
docade. Henco, evon if Japan were forced to a much higher level
of war activity, a considcrable period would elapse before
roliance would havo to be placed upon current production.
(4) Japan has achieved goographic positions both on the
continent and in thc island ardas of the wostcrn Pacific which
give maximum protection to the heart of tho Japenoso cconomic
systom. The heart of this sytem and the essontial elemonts of
Japan's var potential'are concontrated in a northern Innor Zono,
embracing Japan Proper, Koroa, Manchuria, North China and
Formosa.

STE CNGENIRATTONOF JAPANS EOCNOMTOPOTENTEEEG
Tow Japan ia coheentrating on a Long-term program designed to
make the inner sone self-sufficient for war purposes Thsre
is a popular impression that the occupation of the rich raw
materials area in Southeast Asia led to a suspension of this
program. On the contrary, the raw materials in the inner zone
have been exploited more intensively. The building of synthetic
oil plants, the utilization of aluminum shale, and the extrao-
tion of iron ore, for example, have all been promoted in order
to increase self-sufficiency in this area. At the same time
the development of basic processing capacity already centered
here has been carried on as rapidly as possiblo.
As a result of these policies the continental areas near
Japan (Korea, Manchuria, and North China) have become a very
substantial part of Japan's total war cconomy. In the process-
ing industries important in war (particularly iron and steel),
these areas have become so closoly integrated with industry in
Japan Proper as to constitute an indispensable part of the whole.
The following summary indicates (1) the growing depondence of
Japan Proper on the other parts of the inner zone and, (2) the
remarkable dogree of sclf-sufficiency which has now boen achieved
within the innor zone.
Proccssing Capacity
Well over 90 percont of the processing capacity of the
groater East Asia sphere is concentrated in tho north. The con-
centration of war industry in the Innor Zono is almost complcte.
Heavy industry, moreover, is no longer centered ālmost
entiroly in Japan Proper. A complete picturc of tho.industrial
plants in Manchuria and Koroa is not available, but enough is
known to indicate a substantial incroase in Japan's depondence
on the continent's processing capacity during rocont years.
This 'dopendence is most markod in the iron and steel industry.
In other fiolds, such as the production of chomicals, tho pro-
cessing of bauxite and alum shales, the output of ordnance,
synthetic and shalo oil production, and assombly (and partial
manufacture) of airplanes, tanks and trucks, Japan's reliance
on the rest of the Inner Zone has also incroasod.
Figures on eloctric pover installations afford a rough
moasure of tho Manohurian-Korean-Formosan contribution: to the
Innor Zono's procossing capacity. Of total installed capecity
of roughly 17,000,000 kilowatts, Japan Propor has 77.7 porcent,

During 1943 rolling mill capacity will continue to be the
main limitation on steel production. Some increase will be
attained, but at a slow rate. By the end of 1943 the present
annual rate of 6,300,000 tons of steel for finishod products
may possibly have reached a rate of 7,000,000 tons. Of the

In summary, the processing capacity of the iron and steel
industry is concentrated almost 100 percent in the Inner Zone.
Only iron ore, and that in small amounts, comes from the Outer
Zone. Under pressure tho steel industry could dispense with
these southern ores and still maintain production at current
or even considcrably increased rates.
In this industry, Japan Proper is heavily dependent on
the continental parts of the Inner Zone. Menchuria, Korea and
North China supply 93.5 percent of the Inner Zone's..imon ore,
43.5 percent of its pig iron, 20 percent of its gross steel
ingots, and 19.6 percent of its semi-finished stecl.
Coal
A third basic economic index is coal. The Inner Zone
supplies virtually the whole of the Grcater East Asia sphere's
output of coal--that is, roughly 100,000,000 tons out of a total
of possibly 112,000,000 tons. Japan Proper supplies 52.1 per-
cent, tho rest of the Inner Zone 41.4 pcrcent, and the Outer
Zono only 6.5 pcrcent.
In this connoction, it should be noted that Japan's total
consumption of coking coal is supplied by the Inner Zone.
Japen's depondonce on thc continental soctor of thc Innor Zono
is ineroascd by the fact .that large amounts of coking coal are
dorivod from North China. Also,: moro then half of the coko oven
capacity is on the continent-Japan Proper supplying 45.8 percent,
Manahuria 49.9 percont, and Korea 4.3 percent.

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
-88-
Other Economic Potential
Salt. Production of salt (basic for a number of important
chemicals) is also largely concentrated in the Inner Zone. Out
of an estimated 1943 consumption of 2,650,000 tons in Japan
Proper, the Inner Zone will contribute 85.7 percent. Of this
amount Japan Proper will supply 22.6 percent, and the rest of
the Inner Zone 64.1 percent.
Foodstuffs. The Inner Zone has been traditionally self-
sufficient in foodstuffs. Although the food supply has growm
tighter in the last few years, the basic situation has not been
essentially eltered. In 1943, estimated on the basis of a poor
Korean crop, the Inner Zone will supply 91.8 percent of Japan
Proper's consumption of the food staple--rice. Of this amount,
Japan Proper will supply 84.9 percent, leaving only 6.9 percent
to be supplied by Formosa and Korea. In a normal year, Korea
would supply a greater amount. There is also a large stockpile
of rice in Japan Proper.
Transport. Should Japan be forced to withdraw into the
Inner Zone, its lines of communication would be greatly shortened.
The shipping deficit would be turned into a shipping surplus,
although heavier attacks from nearer United Nations bases might
make this result temporary. The Inner Zone, moreover, is
provided with the best land transport facilities of the G.E.A.
sphere. It contains roughly 65 percent of the railway mileage
and more than 75 percent of the rolling stock of the hole
sphere..

I have the honor to enclose copies of a number 
Japanese commercial messages intercepted locally betw
August 4 and 17, 1943, by 0"I. These messages are fo
ed in the hope that they may be of interest to th
Department and to other Government agencies, includin
OE
The local OWI director states that the work of inter
ge sing Japanese commercial messages is still in the pre-
hary stage here; that only from 1,000 to 2,500 words
anbe copied daily, owing to lack of adequate number of
pecaivers and personnel; and that only a few stations can
be vovered for several hours daily. llany of the messages
894.50/216
arfiled by landline or radio circuits from the point of
origin to the despatching point and therefore the first
prace name may represent the despatching point rather
than the point of origin.
It is to be noted that the word "base" occurs in
several messages. OWI translators state that this is
the translation of the Japanese word "kichi", used to
designate a military or naval base. Japanese news des-
patches often carry the date-line "maru-maru kichi", meaning
"a certain base".
If material of this sort lacks practical value and
interest, the Embassy would appreciate being so informed.

1c Mitkokucho (Head  itsui Rice Department?) Tokyo
No. 299. The prevention of short weight is an important
question both to you and us. e have been worting hard on
various counter measures. As a result of consultations
with acting manager Sunayama the following policy has
been determined and we are making e very effort to carry
it out:
1. Upon the arrival of Ir. Ishida at his post the bad
control and malpractices at the rice cleaning mills have
been gredually rectified. But in view of the fact that
there may be e te ptation to...(omission)...We he ve once
more made a strong request to the FIc authorit ies to the
effect that an announcement should be made to all rice
cleaning mills that they must naintain a standard of
100 kilos net weight. At the same time we are consider-
ing measures to iniprove the quality of the persons in
charge of the weighing mechines and to give these jobs to
Jananese supervisors.
2. Short wei ht occuring while in storare at our ware-
houses:
(a) The moisture contained in rice will evaporate
but there are variations according to cl imate and
season. As soon as we he ve investigated this
closely and reckoned a fiyed standard we will
have it verified officially and notify you of
the results.
(b) Damage done by insects and rats. The equipment
of the warehouses and methods of storing should
be improved so as to reduce it gradually.
(c) Short weight due to the nigo (impurities?) in the
warehouses. In the past we have followed the
local habits. After" the rice has been taken from
the warehouses the impurit ies are sifted out and
since it is very hard to tie up the big months of
the harubyo (?) after they heve been well filled
in such a way as to prevent all leakage...
(omission)...Further since the main warehouse at
Saigon has a concrete floor we want to sweep out
the nigo (?) with the chaff. As soon as we get
sleepers we will carry it out.
(a)

=== Page 256 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
(a) A searching examination will be made as to
the rate of shortage occuring during storage
in the warehouses. Twice verifications have
been issued by Consul Kuwadara, on June 19 and
July 19, after the weighing when taken from the
warehouse. But it is still necessary to make
a closer examination as to whether there is any
short weight due to change of season and whether
such shortages occuring in storage after the
weighing in vary with the different types of rice.
3. Short weight due to theft occurs on the canal on the
way for shipment in company ships. Although preventive
measures are under consideration as to control by FIC
authorities such as the reconstruction of the interior
structure of the sampans, sealing the sampan loads and
special supervisors to stay aboard on every trip, neverthe-
less we do not think they will do any good unless trans-
port between Cholon and Saigon is placed entirely under
Japanese manegement. We are giving the question our
closest attention.
4. With regard to the management of No. 2 bags in the
sampans lying alongside company ships, up to now we have
treated them as karubyo (?). But since they are hard to
tie up, from now on ve will distribute mat-rush bags and
kee (?) bags to each samnan end after the nacking...
(omission)... we will he ve them han ded over right along-
side instead. At each loading the comnany steamer will
send us a receipt by the semnan with regard to the No. 2
bags.
We hope to ha ve the comnany line's cooperation and
understand ing on this which we heve asked for during
today's transportation conference. Please convey to the
director of the transportation denartment and to the
officials at all the unloading places these actual con-
ditions at (our?) shipnine place. At the same time while
I hear the increase in short weight due to bad packing,
the enforced hurried loading of shins recently, the low
character of coolies employed, the unkind treatment meted
out by the employers, and the labor shortages at the
ports is somewhat serious, you can imagine that at a
port which has never been used to handdling rice it is
something terrible. I beg that the officials at all
the ports who are faced with these difficulties will send
us their recommendations. You will hear further details
from Mr. Sunayama.

Commeroial Traffic on September 13, 1943

lo Cicei Hanoi
We have received a letter on the question of investigating
copper ores. The formation of investigation group should
be based upon the policy of the governing body and the
regulations, eto. There is no necessity for paying any
attention to objections on the part of the technical
experts. If the investigation group is divided into two
parties, the areas for investigation will be different
from each other and naturally this will lead to disagree-
ments as to the results of the investigations with each
group wanting to get the upper hand. What is to bo the
number of the group must be decided by the actual ciroum-
stances in the actual place. In view of the situation,
criticism in abstract is meaningless. In the first place,
the important thing is to make this investigation of the
kinds of minerals. The technical experts must be under a
reasonable command. Please join by all means this group
together with technioal expert Mr. Nakazawa. When the
investigation group from Japan is organized we will negoti-
ate with you again on the question of the personnel arrange-
ment in future. We beg everyone to put forth a united
front to do his public duty.
Taitaku
Fortbayard

-2-
On September 25, 1943, the Dutch Ambassador informed
an officer of the Embassy that his previous estimate of
the amount of oil the Japanese were obtaining from the
N.E.I. and north Borneo, amounting to 10 per cent of
normal pre-war production, was made in April, 1943. He
stated that now the Japanese are probably obtaining more
oil, as their initial difficulties in restoring instal-
lations in certain areas appeared to have been overcome.
Mr. Lovink added that, however, he did not believe that
current production approximates 50 per cent of pre-war
production. He expressed the opinion that the longer the
Japanese are permitted to occupy the N.E.I. and north
Borneo the more successful they will be in their efforts
to restore oil production facilities, upon which they
are concentrating their energies.

Embassy that he had no information on the subject and
efforts made to obtain this information from Chinese
sources at Chungking have thus far not been successful.
The Embassy will continue its efforts to obtain the
desired information.

Enclosure no. 1 to despatch no. 1635, dated
September 28, 1943, from the Embassy at
Chungking, China.
DEMOLITION OF THE OILFIELDS AND REFINERIES
OF THE ROYAL DUTCH SHELL GROUP IN TIE N.E.I.
From the information which has been received about
the destruction of the oilfields and refineries of the
Royal Dutch Shell Group in the Netherlands East Indies,
the following summary has been compiled.
The Group possessed in the N.E.I. four refinery centers -
Balikpapan
on the island of Borneo
Pangkalan Brandan
on the island of Sumatra
Pladjoe
on the island of Sumatra
Tjepoe/Wonokromo
on the island of Java.
Around these refinery centers a varying number of oil
fields were situated, whereas apart from the above the Group
had exploitation oilfields on the island of Tarakan (N. E.
Borneo) where there was no refinery; an oil exploitation
of minor importance on the island of Ceram (Boela), also
without a refinery, and finally a 40% share in and the
management of the N.N.G.P.M. Company in Mew Guinea, where
a certain amount of oil was found, but which was still in
the exploration stage.
Already a lonE time before the outbreak of the war in
the Far East, detailed plans for dectruction in the shortest
possible time of the Group's refineries and oilfields 1.. the
N.E.I. had been prepared.
In order to make sure that the destruction could actually
be carried out in the time ollotted for it, dynamite charges
were prepared beforehand and the necessary fuse wires had
been laià out in duplicate to the various refining instal-
lations and their auxiliary plants such as water pump stations,
electric power plants, tanks etc.
With regard to the fields, for the same reason the tubings
of the pumping wells had beforehand been raised and suspended
on clamps, so that they could be dropped into the wells simply
by knocking away the clamps with sledge hammers.
Similarly with a view to being able to make e complete
job of it, and since it was realized that pluging ell the
flowing wells with cement at the last moment would take too
much time, a large number of flowing wells had been plugged
with cement beforehand, whilst the required production was
extracted by over-producing the remaining ones so far as was
possible. The tubings in the former wells were also sus-
pended in clamps.
When the Japanese moved forward and the installations
were threatened by the approaching enemy, the planned demol-
ition was carried out on the instructions of the Authorities.
In the refineries the various installations, tanks, etc.
were blown up by means of the dynamite placed in readiness,
and buildings and stocks were set alight. In the fields
the wells which were still producing were plugged with
cement and all welks were then junked, by dropping the
tubings

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
tubings into the wells and on top thereof scrap iron, dril-
ling bits, nails, etc. In some cases charges of trotyl were
dropped on top of the tubings, so that even the casings
were destroyed.
From reports received, and from a number of eyewitnesses
the following particulars can be given with regard to the
extent of the destruction.
Balikpapan (S.E. Borneo)
It is known from eyewitnesses that all the oil wells
were destroyed according to plan; after the wells were
junked, the casings of many wells were blown up with
dynamite. Transportation pumps, as compressors, drilling
equipment etc. were destroyed by dynamite. All other
machinery, motors, cranes, etc. were rendered useless by
removing the vital parts which were smashed by sledge
hammers. The pipelines between the oilfields and the
Refinery center were blown up at several points.
The Balikpapan refinery center was completely des-
troyed. All refinery installations (distilling, cracking,
wax, lubricating oil plants, etc.) with their stills and
boiler houses were destroyed by dynamite or fire; equally
all tanks, storehouses, shops and machinery. The jetties
which were all of wood construction were set on fire after
pumping gasoline and kerosene on them and on the surrounding
water. The stores of packed products were set on fire and
destroyed. Generel stores were blasted and the offices and
similar buildings were set alight. All records, files and
documents were destroyed. Part of the living quarters also
caught fire and was destroyed.
Pangkalan Brandan fields and Refinery (N. Sumatra)
Information was passed on to our management in Batavia
that all the destruction operations for the oilfields and
for the Refinery have been carried out according to plan.
Pladjoe (S. Sumatra)
Eyewitness accounts have also come to hand regarding
the destruction of the Pladjoe fields and refinery instal-
lations. It is reported that all the wells were destroyed
according to plan. of the drilling equipment, destruction
of draw works, engines, slush pumps, boilers and storehouse
spare parts was completed. The pipeline pumping stations
and boiler batteries, the water pump stations, etc. were
destroyed, whereas all records and documents were burnt.
The reports add that all the destruction work in the Plad-
joe fields was carried out according to plan and no material
of any use was left. The pipelines between the fields and
the refinery were blown up at several points.
In Pladjoe Japanese paratroops were dropped right in the
middle of the Refinery center. During the heavy fighting
that followed, some of the installations and tanks caught
fire, but there was still hope that we could hold the refinery;
however,

=== Page 275 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
however, when Japanese reinforcements were arriving up
the river it was realized that the Refinery could no
longer be held and then instructions were received from
Authorities to start destruction operations.
On account of the surprise attack of the Japanese para-
troops, the destruction of the refinery could not be carried
out to plan. However, from reports of eyewitnesses it is
known that the central power plant was destroyed and that the
furnaces of the cracking plant and the central boiler plant
were destroyed by keeping the fires burning and shutting off
oil respectively water inlet. The jetties were partly des-
troyed and many houses and buildings were set on fire. Other
installations were afterwards shelled with mortars. Files,
records and technical data had previously been gathered in
one building, which was set alight. After the evacuation
had taken place, the Refinery center was bombed several
times by Ailied bombers, and it is reported that among
others the Alkylation plant was destroyed by these attacks.
Tjepoe/wonokromo (Java)
The measures for destruction of the refinery instal-
lations and of the oilfields were carried out according to
plan.
Tarakan (N.E. Borneo)
It was reported by eyewitnesses that in the Tarakan oil-
fields all the oilwells were destroyed according to plan;
after the wells were junked the casings in many wells were
blown up with dynamite. The main tankfarm was set alight,
and the central pumping towers, transportation pumps, machine
shops, electric stations, etc., were wrecked by explosives.
All stocks in the stores were destroyed by various means; in-
struments were smashed by sledge hammers, heavier equipment
was demolished with dynamite and the buildings were set alight.
All records, files and documents were destroyed.
Boela (Ceram)
The destruction of the small oilfields on this island
was completed to plan.
New Guinea
The activities of the Group there were still confined to
exploration. The essential exploration wells have been des-
troyed. The greater part of the drilling material had
previously been transported from the fields to the center of
exploration activities at Babo. All equipment and machinery
which could not be removed to Babo has been made useless on
the spot.
At Babo the aerodrome, jetty, electric power plant, wire-
less station, water tanks, oil and petroleum stocks, all
machinery pumps and motors were destroyed. The drilling
equipment, spare parts and remaining material were dumped off
the jetty in 12 metres deep water, together with locomotives,
lorries and road building equipment.
Summarizing

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Summarizing it can be said that practically the whole
of the Group's oil indus try in the N.E.I. has been completely
wrecked, as well refineries as fields, where some 3,O00 wells
were junked or rendered useless to the enemy; further tanks
and product-stores, and all auxiliary installations like
work-shops, and store houses with all their general stores,
drilling equipment and all kinds of machinery.
As an example of how far the destruction was carried
out, a report is attached on the demolition of one of the
important fields in the Pladjoe area.
Enclosure.
LONDON, October 1942.

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 2 to despatch no. 1635, dated
September 28, 1943, from the Embassy at
Chungking, China.
REPORT ON DEMOLITION OF TALANG DJIMAR FIELD 1942. (PLADJOE)
The net result of the demolition is that of a total of
92 wells, all but three were definitely destroyed. Of this
total 49 had been cemented prior to February 15th and pre-
pared for rapid completion by the familiar method of plug-
ging in the casing and supporting the raised tubing in a
clamp. of the remainder, 38 were cemented with the tubing
in position on February 14th and 15th working from several
test plateaux simultaneously. Due to shortage of time two
wells were dynamited and set on fire.
The detailed information appended is divided into two
parts, the first is compiled from definite written records
and the second "reported part" has been recalled from con-
versation with other members of the "Vernielingsgroep" who
carried out that part.
Of the drilling equipment, destruction of draw-works,
engines, slush pumps, boilers and storehouse spare parts
was completed.
The destruction of the pipeline pumping station at
Prabamoeli and boiler settin was completed. The water
pump station at Talang Djimar was similarly completely
destroyed.
The destruction of the Schlumberger electric logging
and gun perforating equipment was completed and the essential
potentiometer removed.
All records in the Prabamoelih office were burnt.
EXPLORATION WELLS:
(1) Logan 1: was plugged in the casing and junk dropped in
the well. Draw-work, boilers and pumps were destroyed as
planned.
(2) Benoeang 2: was plugged and destruction of equipment
reported to te complete.
(3) Goenoeang Kemala 5: had been cemented in the casing in
December, 194l; residual equipment was dynamited.
(4) Other fields: The fields at Limau and Soeban Djerigi were
completely cemented prior to February 15th. There was no
reason why such destruction should not have been completed.
DEFINITE INFORMATION:
(1) Wells cemented prior to February 15th with tubing raised
in a clamp. Destruction was completed by dropping the tubing
followed by various nipples, small material and finally a
reversed rock-bit.

dlt Stilwater Tokyo
No.5. Referring to your telegram of 15th, we will...
hereafter although no insurance business was in operation
in past time. Due to the present status of sea and land,
there will be increased demand for sea going junks sailing
for northern ports and the prices of ships have become
five or six times more than before. However we imagine
the moral risk may be comparatively slight on account of
the difficulty of getting lumber and other kinds of
materials. No brokers undertake insurance business, but
they are carrying business of junk transportation. They
hire ships from many shipowners and possess a considera-
ble sum of...(?) likely. We are making investigation for
their credit condition. The local ship owners do nothing
about business; they leave all gene ral affairs to the bro-
kers. Sixty percent of goods loaded on junk...(?) Inspec-
tion is done only at the time immediately after the
completion of construction work in order to get transport
permit license. These brokers often undertake the process
of inspection. If costs are guaranteed they will work hard
to build any number of junks here and it is perfectly
(certain?) that more than 150 junks have been completed.
The rate of insurance for junks is 10-0/0 150-0 (10%-15%?)
for one year...Please recognize this rate. But the non-
insurable period is October and November when navigation
is impossible (in junks) on account of the seasonal winds,
hence it is the time to...(?) Since the destination is
along the coast there is no hope of applying this non-
insurable period. Please renly soon as we have to make
competition with other firms.
Tokyokaijo

into Thailand became effective on the 18th. But as regards

suspended for the time being at the abovementioned three
branches: (1) the drawing of money orders for places outside
Malaya; (2) remittances from overseas Chinese.

dlt Shionogi Osake
No.58/sm. Although the Embassy has already considered the
point there is still no promising firm to be taken as
applicant. The firms in the northern part are afraid of
being transferred. Since the policy of the authorities
concerned is based on the prineiple that firms in the north-
ern or southern parts should all remain at their respective
places, therefore the intrusion of northern firms into the
southern part, and vice versa, is disallowed. Now a com-
promise plan hes been established, whereby all commodities
are to be unloaded at Saigon and both parties are con-
sidered as being qualified in the northern part. Thus the
establishment of an office is absolutely necessary in order
to transport the goods that arrive to the northern part.
Moreover, if the compromise measure is enforced, we can carry
on our business in a dignified manner with formal qualifi-
cations. For the portion for Dainan we have absolute priority
in unloading the goods for Hanoi at Saigon (according to the
common agreement), but draw as much as possible our bills
at Hanoi.
Shionogi

Note by OWI, Chungking: In the second from the
last telegram above we have a word meaning "sea"
(in that case kai) followed by maru meaning
"blank". In the last telegram, umi likewise means
"sea" and it also has maru combined therewith.
It is possible that kai-maru and umi-maru may be
crude paraphrases for kaigun (navy) in order to
avoid use of the character gun (army) which is
evidently taboo.

purposes) is often torn off, a label indicating "circulation

Consequently, the labor and expenditure have thus been
wasted. Is it possible to ship the box with tinplate so
